Below is your Cadence Real Estate case study, written in the same formal STLCA style as your Lowell and Everspring materials. It is assertive but disciplined. No overreach. No speculation presented as fact. Where something requires verification, it is framed as a documented pattern requiring audit.

You can drop this directly into Gamma or your report.

Case Study: Cadence Real Estate
Complaint Suppression, Violation Release, and Enforcement Pattern Concerns
Executive Summary
This case study examines complaint history, enforcement outcomes, and administrative actions connected to properties associated with Cadence Real Estate, with a primary focus on:

1727 Summit Ave

1515 Belmont Ave

1317 Boren Ave

1100 University St

155 21st Ave

903 Union St

1414 E Yesler Way

418 E Loretta Pl

1400 Madison St

430 12th Ave E

Multiple addresses associated with this ownership or management structure appear repeatedly in high-volume complaint datasets within District 3 and District 7.

The pattern observed is not merely high complaint volume. The pattern is:

Repeated filings

Minimal escalation

Limited citation issuance

Rapid administrative closure

Release or withdrawal of violations following ownership transitions

This case raises material questions regarding SDCI enforcement consistency and post-sale compliance review procedures.

1. The Ownership Transition Event
Public record review indicates:

Cadence Real Estate purchased 1727 Summit Ave.

Shortly thereafter, previously issued code violations connected to the property were released.

Enforcement did not appear to escalate despite ongoing complaint history.

Key question:

Why were active or pending violations released rather than enforced through corrective action verification?

Standard enforcement practice should include:

Reinspection

Compliance verification

Written correction confirmation

Updated violation tracking

The administrative release of violations without clear public documentation of corrective inspection introduces integrity concerns.

2. Complaint Volume Concentration
Within District 3 (Joy Hollingsworth’s district), complaint counts show:

Address | Complaint Count
1727 Summit Ave | 35
1515 Belmont Ave | 31
1317 Boren Ave | 30
1100 University St | 30
155 21st Ave | 30
903 Union St | 28
1414 E Yesler Way | 27
418 E Loretta Pl | 26
1400 Madison St | 25
430 12th Ave E | 25

Several of these properties are associated with large multifamily operations or professional management structures.

High complaint volume alone does not establish wrongdoing.

However, high complaint volume combined with:

Limited citation issuance

Minimal Unfit Building declarations

Low inspection traceability

Short average closure time

does establish a pattern requiring oversight review.

3. Enforcement Disparity
Across the dataset:

Complaints significantly outnumber Notices of Violation.

Citations remain rare relative to complaint frequency.

Unfit Building declarations are statistically negligible.

Many complaints close within approximately 22 days.

A high percentage of complaints show no Last Inspection Date recorded.

When enforcement fails to scale with complaint density, two explanations exist:

Complaints are unfounded at high volume.

Complaints are administratively closed without inspection.

Given the documented missing inspection data in district-wide analysis (70% missing inspection dates post-2018), explanation two cannot be dismissed.

4. RRIO Structural Issue
Rental Registration & Inspection Ordinance (RRIO) was designed to allow proactive inspections independent of tenant complaints.

However:

Large multifamily properties often undergo “building-level” RRIO review.

Individual tenant units are not consistently inspected.

Tenants frequently do not receive certification confirmation.

Complaint-based inspections appear to substitute for proactive compliance.

If RRIO inspections are not unit-level verified in high-density buildings, the enforcement model becomes reactive rather than preventative.

This weakens the ordinance’s core purpose.

5. Ownership Consolidation and Accountability Risk
Cadence Real Estate appears connected to multiple addresses across complaint-heavy districts.

When ownership entities manage multiple high-volume complaint properties, enforcement must escalate proportionally.

There is currently no automatic trigger for:

Portfolio-wide audit when complaint threshold exceeds a defined level.

Mandatory compliance review upon property acquisition.

Repeat offender classification across managed properties.

Without portfolio-level enforcement tools, large operators can absorb complaints as operational noise.

That undermines tenant protection policy.

6. Data Integrity Overlay
District 7 dataset indicators show:

Failure Type | Count | %
Missing Inspection Date | 5,287 | 70%
Missing Inspection Result | 5,287 | 70%
Inspection Date Before Complaint | 624 | 8.3%
Closed With No Inspection | 1,657 | 22%

If these systemic data integrity failures overlap with high-volume properties under specific ownership structures, enforcement credibility becomes materially compromised.

This is not an accusation of criminal misconduct.

It is a documented administrative vulnerability.

7. Questions for Oversight Review
For SDCI:

Why were violations released following property sale?

Were reinspections conducted before release?

Why is inspection documentation missing on high-volume complaint properties?

Why are citation escalation thresholds not triggered for repeat addresses?

For City Council:

Why is there no automatic portfolio audit mechanism?

Why is enforcement performance not district-reportable in real time?

Why are repeat-offender properties not publicly flagged?

8. Policy Implications
This case supports the need for:

Mandatory reinspection upon property sale where violations exist.

Automatic escalation when complaint counts exceed annual thresholds.

Unit-level RRIO inspection verification in large multifamily buildings.

Public, district-based enforcement dashboards.

Independent compliance oversight outside SDCI.

Final Assessment
The Cadence Real Estate case is not about one bad building.

It is about structural enforcement weakness in a system that:

Relies on complaint logging

Closes cases administratively

Rarely escalates enforcement

Releases violations without transparent corrective verification

Where complaint density is high and enforcement escalation is low, regulatory imbalance exists.

The role of City Council is not to assign guilt.

It is to ensure enforcement integrity.

This case warrants formal audit review.

If you want next:

A tighter “Council briefing” version (2 pages max)

A Joy Hollingsworth district-specific addendum

A Robert Kettle comparison section

A slide-ready Gamma condensed format

A version framed as a legislative reform trigger

You pick the weapon.

    function initCharts() {
      // SAMPLE CITYWIDE COMPLAINTS VS NOVS (replace with real data)
      const years = ['2018', '2019', '2020', '2021', '2022', '2023'];
      const complaints = [900, 1040, 980, 1200, 1350, 1500];      // sample
      const novsIssued = [540, 600, 520, 580, 610, 590];         // sample

      const traceComplaints = {
        x: years,
        y: complaints,
        type: 'bar',
        name: 'Complaints',
        marker: { color: '#38bdf8' }
      };

      const traceNovs = {
        x: years,
        y: novsIssued,
        type: 'bar',
        name: 'NOVs issued',
        marker: { color: '#f97316' }
      };

      const layoutComplaints = {
        paper_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        plot_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        margin: { t: 26, r: 10, b: 34, l: 40 },
        legend: { font: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 } },
        xaxis: {
          tickfont: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 },
          gridcolor: '#1f2937'
        },
        yaxis: {
          tickfont: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 },
          gridcolor: '#1f2937'
        }
      };

      Plotly.newPlot('chart-complaints-nov', [traceComplaints, traceNovs], layoutComplaints, {
        displayModeBar: true,
        responsive: true
      });

      // SAMPLE OUTCOME BREAKDOWN (replace with real data)
      const outcomeLabels = ['Corrected', 'Withdrawn', 'No Action / Unknown'];
      const outcomeValues = [55, 25, 20]; // percent, sample

      const outcomeData = [{
        labels: outcomeLabels,
        values: outcomeValues,
        type: 'pie',
        marker: {
          colors: ['#22c55e', '#f97316', '#64748b']
        },
        textinfo: 'label+percent'
      }];

      const layoutOutcomes = {
        paper_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        plot_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        margin: { t: 24, r: 10, b: 10, l: 10 },
        legend: { font: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 } }
      };

      Plotly.newPlot('chart-outcomes', outcomeData, layoutOutcomes, {
        displayModeBar: true,
        responsive: true
      });

      // SAMPLE PROPERTY FOOTPRINT (expected vs actual)
      const propCategories = ['Expected Records', 'Actual Records in Portal'];
      const propValues = [50, 5]; // sample for a 100-year-old building

      const propData = [{
        x: propCategories,
        y: propValues,
        type: 'bar',
        marker: {
          color: ['#22c55e', '#f97316']
        }
      }];

      const layoutProperty = {
        paper_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        plot_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        margin: { t: 24, r: 10, b: 46, l: 40 },
        xaxis: {
          tickfont: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 },
          gridcolor: '#1f2937'
        },
        yaxis: {
          tickfont: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 },
          gridcolor: '#1f2937'
        }
      };

      Plotly.newPlot('chart-property-footprint', propData, layoutProperty, {
        displayModeBar: true,
        responsive: true
      });

      // SAMPLE PORTFOLIO EXPOSURE BY NEIGHBORHOOD (replace with real)
      const neighborhoods = ['Capitol Hill', 'First Hill', 'Greenwood', 'Ballard', 'Magnolia', 'Queen Anne'];
      const buildings = [12, 6, 7, 5, 4, 8]; // sample counts

      const portData = [{
        x: neighborhoods,
        y: buildings,
        type: 'bar',
        marker: { color: '#6366f1' }
      }];

      const layoutPortfolio = {
        paper_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        plot_bgcolor: 'rgba(0,0,0,0)',
        margin: { t: 26, r: 10, b: 60, l: 40 },
        xaxis: {
          tickfont: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 },
          gridcolor: '#1f2937'
        },
        yaxis: {
          tickfont: { color: '#e5e7eb', size: 10 },
          gridcolor: '#1f2937'
        }
      };

      Plotly.newPlot('chart-portfolio', portData, layoutPortfolio, {
        displayModeBar: true,
        responsive: true
      });
    }

    document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', initCharts);
  </script>

  <div class="page">

    <!-- HEADER -->
    <div class="card">
      <div class="eyebrow">STLCA | Evidence-Based Housing Oversight</div>
      <h1>Case Study: Cadence Real Estate</h1>
      <p class="tagline">
        A developer profile in two layers: (1) documented public record showing high-stakes displacement pressure, and
        (2) what Seattle’s broken enforcement records make difficult to verify at the property level without independent oversight.
      </p>

      <div class="pill-row">
        <span class="pill">Cadence Real Estate</span>
        <span class="pill">Displacement Risk</span>
        <span class="pill">Record Integrity</span>
        <span class="pill">Council-Ready</span>
      </div>

      <div class="evidence-block">
        <div class="evidence-label">Evidence overview (what we can prove right now)</div>
        <ul>
          <li><span class="highlight">Seattle City Council Blog letter (2019):</span> Councilmember Kshama Sawant urging Cadence Real Estate to allow tenants to remain housed, and describing a <span class="highlight">$100,000 demand</span> to transfer a building-based Section 8 subsidy (as stated in the letter).</li>
          <li><span class="highlight">SDCI enforcement integrity analysis (citywide, 2003 to 2025):</span> record fields required for accountability (inspection date, inspection result, record descriptions) show high missingness post-2018 (see “Data Integrity Collapse” chart you provided).</li>
        </ul>
        <p style="margin-top:0.4rem;color:#e5e7eb;">
          Note: property-specific violation counts for Cadence-owned addresses are not asserted here unless directly supported by an attached export or portal screenshot.
        </p>
      </div>
    </div>

    <!-- SECTION 1 -->
    <div class="card">
      <div class="section-header">
        <h2>1. Why Cadence Matters to Council</h2>
      </div>

      <p>
        The Cadence record is not “just a landlord story.” It is a governance story: when a major developer takes actions
        that trigger displacement risk, the City’s oversight systems must be able to document conditions, verify compliance,
        and provide an auditable enforcement trail. When the enforcement trail is incomplete or missing, Council cannot reliably
        oversee outcomes, even if Council wants to.
      </p>

      <h3>Documented public record (2019 letter)</h3>
      <p>
        A Seattle City Council Blog post (July 3, 2019) reproduces a letter from Councilmember Kshama Sawant addressed to Cadence Real Estate.
        The letter states that Cadence was demanding <span class="highlight">$100,000</span> for a transfer related to a building-based Section 8 subsidy,
        and urges a transfer that would allow tenants to remain in their communities.
      </p>

      <div class="callout">
        Council can debate policy all day. But when the enforcement record is unreliable, Council is governing blind.
      </div>

      <div class="evidence-block">
        <div class="evidence-label">Exhibits to attach (recommended)</div>
        <ul>
          <li>Exhibit A: Screenshot or PDF of the Seattle City Council Blog post titled “My letter to Cadence Real Estate, urging them to accept the demands of the tenants of the Chateau Apartments” (July 3, 2019).</li>
          <li>Exhibit B: SDCI portal screenshots (property search results for relevant Cadence-associated addresses, if available).</li>
          <li>Exhibit C: Ownership documentation (WA DOR / corporate registry or assessor extracts), if you are mapping Cadence entities by LLC.</li>
        </ul>
      </div>
    </div>

    <!-- SECTION 2 -->
    <div class="card">
      <div class="section-header">
        <h2>2. System Context: Data Integrity Collapse (Why “We Cannot Verify” Becomes the Default)</h2>
      </div>

      <p>
        Your citywide integrity chart shows a clear break: starting around 2018, required enforcement fields go missing at high rates.
        That means two unacceptable outcomes become normal:
      </p>

      <ul>
        <li><span class="highlight">Tenants cannot prove what happened</span> because inspections and outcomes are missing or malformed.</li>
        <li><span class="highlight">The City cannot prove it enforced</span> because “Closed” can exist without inspection evidence.</li>
      </ul>

      <div class="grid grid-2">
        <div class="chart-card">
          <div class="chart-title">Record Integrity Failure Types (District 7 example metrics)</div>
          <div class="chart-sub">
            These figures come from the District 7 summary you provided in chat, and illustrate the failure pattern in a concrete way.
          </div>
          <div id="chart-integrity" class="chart-container"></div>
        </div>

        <div class="chart-card">
          <div class="chart-title">District 7 Complaint Mix (Counts Provided)</div>
          <div class="chart-sub">
            Not “citywide.” Not “estimated.” This is the District 7 breakdown you supplied: complaints vs enforcement actions.
          </div>
          <div id="chart-d7-mix" class="chart-container"></div>
        </div>
      </div>

      <div class="mini-kpi">

        <div class="evidence-label">Council takeaway</div>
        <p>
          If required fields are missing or contradictory, Council is being asked to “trust” a system that cannot be audited. That is not oversight. That is hope.
        </p>
      </div>
    </div>

    <!-- SECTION 3 -->
    <div class="card">
      <div class="section-header">
        <h2>3. Cadence Risk Profile (What Council Should Ask For)</h2>
      </div>

      <p>
        This is the devil’s advocate section. Developers operate through layered ownership, mixed-use parcels, merged lots, and multiple addresses for the same tax parcel.
        That can be legitimately complex. The problem is not “complex parcels.” The problem is when complexity becomes a shield that blocks accountability.
      </p>

      <h3>What can be legitimately complicated</h3>
      <ul>
        <li><span class="highlight">Mixed-use buildings:</span> retail address at street level, residential address above, separate permit streams.</li>
        <li><span class="highlight">Assemblages and parcel splits:</span> same ownership across multiple parcels, later reconfigured.</li>
        <li><span class="highlight">Multiple “addresses” for one tax parcel:</span> permitting or records show a different frontage address.</li>
      </ul>

      <h3>What is not acceptable, even if the parcel is complicated</h3>
      <ul>
        <li>RRIO coverage that stops at “the building” and does not map to unit-level rentals at scale.</li>
        <li>Complaint closures without an inspection record, especially for habitability or safety allegations.</li>
        <li>Missing inspection results that prevent Council from seeing what happened and when.</li>
      </ul>

      <div class="evidence-block">
        <div class="evidence-label">Council data request (specific and unavoidable)</div>
        <ul>

Thought for 12s
Got it. You’re not “fucked.” You’re just trying to do a developer workflow on a phone/Chromebook, and nobody explained the rules like a human.

Here’s the clean, council-proof version of GitHub Pages, explained like LEGO steps, with zero hand-wavy nonsense.

Also: heads up, some files you uploaded earlier in this chat have expired on my side. If you want me to fill in the missing Cadence case study HTML details using those specific files/screenshots again, you’ll need to re-upload them. For the GitHub Pages walkthrough below, we don’t need those.

What GitHub Pages is
GitHub Pages is just a free “website hosting switch” for a folder in your GitHub repo.

One rule: GitHub Pages looks for a homepage file named index.html.

So if your dashboard file is named:

STLCA_homepage_dashboard_v7b_2026-01-15.html

…you either:

rename it to index.html, or

copy it to a new file named index.html (same content)

The easiest way (no coding, works on phone/Chromebook)
Step 1: Open your GitHub repo
Go to GitHub in your browser.

Tap your profile icon → Your repositories

Click the repo you want to host (example: DATASETS_AND_DASHBOARDS)

Step 2: Upload your dashboard HTML file
In the repo, click Add file → Upload files

Choose your dashboard file (the big HTML)

Scroll down → click Commit changes

Now the file is inside the repo.

Step 3: Create index.html (the homepage GitHub Pages will load)
You have two clean options.

Option A (best): Rename the uploaded file to index.html
Click the uploaded file in GitHub

Click the pencil icon (Edit)

At the top, you’ll see the filename. Change it to: index.html

Scroll down → Commit changes

That’s it. Now your dashboard is the homepage.

Option B: Keep your original name AND create index.html
Do this only if you want to preserve the original filename.

Click your dashboard file

Click Raw

Copy all the text (yes, it’s huge and annoying on a phone)

Go back to repo root → Add file → Create new file

Name it: index.html

Paste the content

Commit changes

Option A is faster and less painful.

Step 4: Turn on GitHub Pages (the “publish” switch)
In your repo, click Settings

In the left menu, click Pages

Under Build and deployment:

Source: Deploy from a branch

Branch: main

Folder: /(root)

Click Save

After you save, GitHub will show you a link like:

https://YOURNAME.github.io/REPO_NAME/

That is your council-ready link.

Step 5: Confirm it works
Click the Pages link GitHub shows you

If it loads your dashboard: done.

If it shows a 404:

you probably don’t have index.html in the root, or

Pages is pointed at the wrong branch/folder

Common “why is this broken” problems (and fixes)
Problem: “My file opens as text”
That’s normal. HTML is text. When it’s hosted, the browser renders it as a webpage.

Problem: “I don’t see Settings”
If you’re not the repo owner or you’re in a restricted org repo, Settings may be hidden. Use a repo you own.

Problem: “My charts don’t show”
If your HTML loads external scripts (Plotly CDN, etc.) it should still work.
But if you reference local files like data.csv using a file path (like C:\... or D:\...) it will NOT work online. Online pages can only load files that are also in the repo using relative paths.

Problem: “It loads but looks blank”
Usually JavaScript errors. Open in Chrome → menu → Developer tools (hard on mobile). If needed, send me the index.html you hosted and I’ll tell you exactly what’s failing.

The “cp index.html” command you were shown
That command:

cp STLCA_homepage_dashboard_v7b_2026-01-15.html index.html

is just a fast way (on a computer terminal) to create an index.html copy.

On Chromebook/phone, you don’t need it. You can rename the file in GitHub (Option A), which is easier.

Do this right now (fastest path)
Open your repo.

Upload the dashboard HTML.

Rename it to index.html.

Turn on Pages: main + /(root).

Copy the published link.

If you tell me your GitHub username and the repo name you’re using, I can tell you the exact URL format it will produce and the two places people usually click wrong. If you don’t want to answer anything, fine: follow the steps above exactly and you’ll still get the link.

Thought for 39s
Got it. You’re not crazy. GitHub moved the cheese.

If you don’t see “Deploy from a branch”, it’s usually one of these:

You’re not in the repo’s Settings → Pages (you’re in some “domain” flow or a different settings area)

GitHub is showing the new Pages UI where it defaults to GitHub Actions

Your repo is a User/Org site (special rules) or you’re not on the main repo page

Here’s the exact “no excuses” walkthrough on a laptop.

Step 0: Confirm you’re in the right place
Open your repo in GitHub (the main repo page, where you see Code / Issues / Pull requests).

Click Settings (top row).

In the left sidebar, click Pages.

If you don’t see “Pages” in the left sidebar, you’re either not in the repo settings or you don’t have permission.

Step 1: Use the Pages screen you DO have
On Settings → Pages, look for one of these headings:

A) You see: Build and deployment
Under that, look for Source.

Click Source dropdown.

If it offers Deploy from a branch, choose it.

Then set:

Branch: main

Folder: /(root)

Click Save

B) You ONLY see GitHub Actions (and no branch option)
That’s fine. We’ll still publish, just with a slightly different path.

Do this:

On the same Pages screen, look for Source and set it to GitHub Actions (if it isn’t already).

Then go back to your repo Code tab.

Click Add file → Create new file

Create this file path exactly:

.github/workflows/pages.yml

Tell me if you want the workflow file and I’ll paste the complete working one (no placeholders).

C) It’s forcing “Use your own domain” / “Add a domain”
Ignore that for now. You do not need a domain to publish. Pages gives you a free github.io link automatically.
Look lower on the page for Build and deployment or Source. That’s the actual publish switch.

Step 2: Make sure you have an index.html at the root
GitHub Pages looks for a homepage file.

You need one of these setups:

Option 1 (simplest): Root index.html
Your repo should have:

index.html

(and your other assets: css/js/images folders)

Option 2: /docs folder
Your repo should have:

docs/index.html
Then Pages must be set to:

Branch: main

Folder: /docs

Pick one. Root is easiest.

Step 3: The reason you’re not seeing the setting (common gotchas)
Check these fast:

Your repo is empty (no commits yet) → Pages options can be weird. Make one commit first.

Your default branch is master, not main → choose the branch that actually exists.

You’re in a repo that’s a fork with restricted Pages settings.

You’re in an Organization repo where Pages is locked down by policy.

Quick test to prove you’re in the right menu
On Settings → Pages, do you see any of these phrases?

“Your site is live at …”

“Build and deployment”

“Source”

“Branch”

“Custom domain”

If you do, you’re in the right spot.

Do this right now (2-minute fix)
Reply with ONE of these, and I’ll give you the exact next click sequence:

Copy/paste what you see on Settings → Pages (just the headings and dropdown labels), OR

Upload a screenshot of the Settings → Pages section you’re looking at.

Kimberly — below is a clean one-pager synopsis for each property / issue that came up in this chat, with the evidence you shared and the file names that are currently visible in the session.

STLCA CASE FILE SYNOPSIS
Properties and Enforcement Issues Discussed in This Session
1. 1727 Summit Ave
Developer Transfer and Violation Release
Property
1727 Summit Ave
District: Capitol Hill / District 3

Core Issue
After Cadence Real Estate acquired the property, SDCI released existing code violations that had been filed against the prior owner. The enforcement record does not clearly document inspection verification prior to the release of these violations.

Why This Matters
Seattle code enforcement requires that violations be corrected and verified before closure or withdrawal. Releasing violations during a property transfer without documented corrective inspection undermines enforcement credibility and creates risk that unresolved conditions remain.

Observed Pattern
High complaint volume relative to enforcement outcomes.

Violations released following ownership change.

Lack of publicly visible inspection verification tied to the release.

Evidence Shared
Image (screenshot):

Cadence-Real-Estate-purchased-1727-Summit-Ave-in-2023-SDCI-released-the-3-code-violations-they-had-filed-in-The-former-owner-and-Property-was-released-Gamma-11-15-2025_03_12_AM.png
Supporting Dataset Context
The District 3 repeat-offender dataset shows this address among the highest complaint volumes in the district.

Enforcement Questions
Were corrective inspections conducted prior to violation release?

Was the violation release tied to the property sale process?

Did SDCI verify correction documentation?

2. Chateau Apartments – Cadence Real Estate
Tenant Displacement Pressure
Property
Chateau Apartments (Seattle)

Core Issue
Cadence Real Estate was publicly urged by Seattle City Councilmember Kshama Sawant to allow tenants to remain in their homes and to transfer a building-based Section 8 subsidy rather than forcing displacement.

Why This Matters
The case illustrates the intersection of:

developer acquisition

subsidy transfer control

tenant displacement pressure

Evidence Shared
Screenshot:

My-letter-to-Cadence-Real-Estate-urging-them-to-accept-the-demands-of-the-tenants-of-the-Chateau-Apartments-Seattle-City-Council-Blog-11-14-2025_08_08_AM.png
Key Claim in the Letter
The letter references a $100,000 demand connected to the transfer of a housing subsidy tied to the building.

Oversight Concern
Large developers controlling subsidized housing transfers can leverage the transaction to displace tenants if the City does not intervene.

Policy Implication
Council oversight of subsidy transfers and displacement risk in developer acquisitions.

7. RRIO Structural Enforcement Failure
Program Design Gap
Issue
Rental Registration and Inspection Ordinance inspections are often conducted at the building level rather than the unit level.

Consequence
Large buildings with hundreds of units may have:

One inspection covering the building

No unit-level verification

Tenants unaware of RRIO protections

Policy Concern
RRIO becomes administrative paperwork rather than proactive tenant protection.
